See
4

Proof.

Using the closed form expressions of
f_{\gamma}
and
\hat{f}_{\gamma}
, write

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\gamma}-f_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\mathbf{y}-(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})f_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\mathbf{y}-n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}f_{\gamma}-\gamma f_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\mathbf{y}-n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}f_{\gamma}-\iota^{*}(f^{\star}-\iota f_{\gamma})).

Here, we have

\lVert(n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\rVert_{\text{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma},

and so

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{f}_{\gamma}-f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma}\lVert\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\mathbf{y}-n\bm{\iota}_{X}^{*}\circ\bm{\iota}_{X}f_{\gamma}-\iota^{*}(f^{\star}-\iota f_{\gamma})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)(y_{i}-f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))-\mathbb{E}[K(\mathbf{x},\cdot)(f^{\star}(\mathbf{x})-f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}))]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}.

Here, define random variables
Z,Z_{i}:\Omega\to\mathcal{H}
by
Z=K(\mathbf{x},\cdot)(f^{\star}(\mathbf{x})-f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}))
and
Z_{i}=K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)(y_{i}-f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))
. Then we have
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[Z]
, and

\lVert\hat{f}_{\gamma}-f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}(Z_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}])\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}.

Hence, we can apply vector-valued Hoeffding’s inequality (
B.2
). First note that, using the reproducing property and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\lvert f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\langle f_{\gamma},K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\iota^{*}\circ\iota+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\iota^{*}f^{\star}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert(\iota^{*}\circ\iota+\gamma\text{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\rVert_{\text{op}}\lVert f^{\star}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma},

where we applied (
1
) on the last line. Then using (
1
), almost surely,

\displaystyle\lVert Z_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lvert y_{i}-f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\rvert\lVert K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\lvert y_{i}\rvert+\lvert f_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\rvert)\lVert K(\mathbf{x}_{i},\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{\gamma}.

We are now ready to apply vector-valued Hoeffding’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\hat{f}_{\gamma}-f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}(Z_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}])\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\gamma n\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma^{2}n^{2}\varepsilon}{16n(1+\frac{1}{\gamma})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}

as required, where we applied Assumption
1
3
.
∎

Missing Details from Section
4

In this section, we provide all the missing details from Section

, including proofs.

Gradient matrix

\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{w})*X^{\top}

NTK

\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\kappa_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\langle G(\mathbf{x}),G(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\langle G_{W}(\mathbf{x}),G_{W}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}

=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}}[\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

=\frac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{m}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}W^{\top})\phi^{\prime}(W\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

NTK Matrix

\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

\mathbf{H}_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

\mathbf{H}=\langle\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}}=

\mathbf{H}_{W}=\mathbf{G}_{W}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{W}=

(XX^{\top})\odot\mathbb{E}[\phi^{\prime}(X\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}^{\top}X^{\top})]

\frac{XX^{\top}}{m}\odot(\phi^{\prime}(XW^{\top})\phi^{\prime}(WX^{\top}))

D.2
NTK Theory of Two-Layer ReLU Networks

In this section, we present a brief development of the theory of neural tangent kernels (NTKs) specific to our model used in Section
4
.

We will consider a two-layer fully-connected neural network with ReLU activation function, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
is the width of the hidden layer. Specifically, write
\phi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the ReLU function defined as
\phi(z)=\max\{0,z\}
, and with a slight abuse of notation, write
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
for the componentwise ReLU function,
\phi(\mathbf{z})=\phi((z_{1},...,z_{m})^{\top})=(\phi(z_{1}),...,\phi(z_{m}))^{\top}
.

Denote by
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
the weight matrix of the hidden layer, by
\mathbf{w}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},j=1,...,m
the
j^{\text{th}}
neuron of the hidden layer and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{m})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
the weights of the output layer. Then for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the output of the network is

f_{W}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\mathbf{a}\cdot\phi\left(W\mathbf{x}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum^{m}_{j=1}a_{j}\phi\left(\sum^{d}_{k=1}W_{jk}x_{k}\right).

For weights
W
, we write
\xi_{W}
noise random variable and
\zeta_{W}
for the error respectively:

\xi_{W}=\xi_{f_{W}}=y-f_{W}(\mathbf{x}):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad\zeta_{W}=\zeta_{f_{W}}=f^{\star}-f_{W}\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}).

Further, we have the following vectors obtained by evaluation at the points
\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
:

\mathbf{f}_{W}=(f_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{1}),...,f_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{n}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad\bm{\xi}_{W}=\bm{\xi}_{f_{W}}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{f}_{W},\qquad\bm{\zeta}_{W}=\bm{\zeta}_{f_{W}}=\mathbf{f}^{\star}-\mathbf{f}_{W}.

First note that, for any
a\geqslant 0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi(az)=a\phi(z)
, a property called
positive homogeneity
.

The ReLU function
\phi
has gradient 0 for
z<0
, gradient 1 for
z>0
and its gradient is undefined at
z=0
. We extend this to a left-continuous function by defining
\phi^{\prime}(z)=\mathbf{1}\{z>0\}
, and treat it as the “gradient” of
\phi
. For higher-dimensional quantities, we extend
\phi^{\prime}
by applying the function componentwise again, i.e.,
\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{z})=\phi^{\prime}((z_{1},...,z_{m})^{\top})=(\phi^{\prime}(z_{1}),...,\phi^{\prime}(z_{m}))^{\top}
, via an abuse of notation.

We define the
gradient function

G_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
at
W
as:

\displaystyle\left[\nabla_{W}f_{W}(\mathbf{x})\right]_{j,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{j}}{\sqrt{m}}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\text{for }j=1,...,m,k=1,...,d,

\displaystyle G_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x})=\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}f_{W}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{j}}{\sqrt{m}}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\text{for }j=1,...,m,

\displaystyle G_{W}(\mathbf{x})=\nabla_{W}f_{W}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\left(\mathbf{a}\odot\phi^{\prime}(W\mathbf{x})\right)\mathbf{x}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}.

We also define the
pre-gradient function

J_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
pre-gradient matrix

\mathbf{J}_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
at
W
based on the sample
X
by the following:

J_{W}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\mathbf{a}\odot\phi^{\prime}(W\mathbf{x}),\qquad\mathbf{J}_{W}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\text{diag}[\mathbf{a}]\phi^{\prime}(WX^{\top}).

Then note that
G_{W}(\mathbf{x})=J_{W}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}^{\top}
, and defining the
gradient matrix

\mathbf{G}_{W}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{J}_{W}*X^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{md\times n}
at
W
, we have

[\mathbf{G}_{W}]_{d(j-1)+k,i}=[\mathbf{J}_{W}]_{j,i}X_{i,k}=\frac{a_{j}}{\sqrt{m}}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{k},

i.e.,the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathbf{G}_{W}
is the vectorization of
\nabla_{W}f_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
, and

[\nabla_{W}f_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{i})]_{j,k}=[\mathbf{G}_{W}]_{d(j-1)+k,i}.

D.2.1
Neural Tangent Kernel

In this section, we collect various definitions and notations related to the
neural tangent kernel
(NTK)
(
Jacot et al. 2018
)
of our network. The notation is consistent with those in Appendix
B.3
.

We define the
neural tangent kernel
(NTK)
\kappa_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
at
W
as the positive semi-definite kernel defined with the gradient function
G_{W}=\nabla_{W}f_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
at
W
as the feature map:

\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\langle G_{W}(\mathbf{x}),G_{W}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}=\frac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\frac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{m}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}W^{\top})\phi^{\prime}(W\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

We also define the
neural tangent kernel Gram matrix
(NTK Gram matrix)
\mathbf{H}_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
at
W
as

\mathbf{H}_{W}=\mathbf{G}^{\top}_{W}\mathbf{G}_{W}=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n})\end{pmatrix},

and write its eigenvalues as
\bm{\lambda}_{W,1}\geqslant...\geqslant\bm{\lambda}_{W,n}=\bm{\lambda}_{W,\min}
in decreasing order (with multiplicity).

Then note that, by (
B.1
), we have

\mathbf{H}_{W}=(\mathbf{J}_{W}*X^{\top})^{\top}(\mathbf{J}_{W}*X^{\top})=(XX^{\top})\odot(\mathbf{J}_{W}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{W})=\frac{1}{m}(XX^{\top})\odot(\phi^{\prime}(XW^{\top})\phi^{\prime}(WX^{\top})).

We can decompose the NTK as a sum of NTK’s corresponding to each neuron. For each
j=1,...,m
, define
\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\frac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{m}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

The NTK matrix also decomposes similarly:

\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n})\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{m}(XX^{\top})\odot(\phi^{\prime}(X\mathbf{w}_{j}^{\top})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}X^{\top})).

Then we have

\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\sum^{m}_{j=1}\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{W}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}.

We denote by
\mathscr{H}_{W}
the RKHS associated with
\kappa_{W}
, and call it the
neural tangent reproducing kernel Hilbert space
(NTRKHS) at
W
. We denote the inner product in this Hilbert space by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{H}_{W}}
and its corresponding norm by
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}_{W}}
.

We denote the
inclusion operator
and its adjoint by

\iota_{W}:\mathscr{H}_{W}\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),\qquad\iota^{*}_{W}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow\mathscr{H}_{W}.

We also have the self-adjoint operator

H_{W}\vcentcolon=\iota_{W}\circ\iota^{\star}_{W}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}).

Again, we consider the neuron-level decomposition. For each
j=1,...,m
, denote by
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}
the NTRKHS corresponding to the NTK
\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}
. Then exactly analogously, we have

\iota_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}:\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),\quad\iota^{\star}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}},\quad H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}=\iota_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\circ\iota^{\star}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),

with
\lVert\iota_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{\text{op}}=\lVert\iota^{\star}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{\text{op}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
and

H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}f(\cdot)=\iota^{\star}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}f(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[f(\mathbf{x})\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)]

for
f\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
. Then

\sum^{m}_{j=1}H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}f(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[f(\mathbf{x})\sum^{m}_{j=1}\kappa_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[f(\mathbf{x})\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)]=H_{W}f(\cdot),

so

H_{W}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}.

We denote the sampling operator and its adjoint based on the i.i.d. copies
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}^{n}_{i=1}
of
\mathbf{x}
by

\bm{\iota}_{W}:\mathscr{H}_{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad\bm{\iota}^{*}_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{H}_{W},

with
\bm{\iota}_{W}\circ\bm{\iota}^{*}_{W}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbf{H}_{W}
(c.f. Appendix
B.3
).

D.2.2
Initialization and Analytical Counterparts

Recall that
m
is an even number; this was to facilitate the popular
antisymmetric initialization trick

(
Zhang et al. 2020
, Section 6)
(see also, for example,
(
Bowman and Montufar 2022
, Section 2.3)
and
(
Montanari and Zhong 2022
, Eqn. (34) & Remark 7(ii))
).

The hidden layer weights are initialized by independent standard Gaussians via the
antisymmetric initialization scheme
,
[W(0)]_{j,k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for
j=1,...,\frac{m}{2}
and
k=1,...,d
. In other words, for each
j=1,...,\frac{m}{2}
,
\mathbf{w}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\mathbf{w}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. The output layer weights
a_{j},j=1,...,\frac{m}{2}
are initialized from
\text{Unif}\{-1,1\}
and are kept fixed throughout training. Then, for
j=\frac{m}{2}+1,...,m
, we let
\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)=\mathbf{w}_{j-\frac{m}{2}}(0)
and
a_{j}=-a_{j-\frac{m}{2}}
. Then we define
f_{W}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(f_{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m/2}}+f_{\mathbf{w}_{m/2+1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m}})
. This ensures that our network at initialization is exactly zero, i.e.,
f_{W(0)}(\mathbf{x})=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, while being able to carry out the analysis as if we had
m
independent neurons distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
at initialization. This is what we do henceforth.

We define the analytical versions of the objects defined earlier by taking the expectation with respect to this initialization distribution of the weights. First, define the
analytical pre-gradient function

J:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{N})
and
analytical pre-gradient matrix

\mathbf{J}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{N})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

J(\mathbf{x})(\mathbf{w})=a(\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}),\qquad\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{w})=a(\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(X\mathbf{w}).

Then define the
analytical gradient function

G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{N})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the
analytical gradient matrix

\mathbf{G}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{N})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

G(\mathbf{x})(\mathbf{w})=J(\mathbf{x})(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{x}=a(\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x},\qquad\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w})=a(\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(X\mathbf{w})*X^{\top}.

Then we have, exactly analogously, the
analytical NTK

\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\langle G(\mathbf{x}),G(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]=\mathbb{E}_{W\sim W(0)}[\kappa_{W}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

and the
analytical NTK matrix

\mathbf{H}

\mathbf{H}=\langle\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\kappa(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\dots&\kappa(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n})\end{pmatrix},

with its eigenvalues denoted as
\bm{\lambda}_{1}\geqslant...\geqslant\bm{\lambda}_{n}=\bm{\lambda}_{\min}
.

We also have the neuron-level decomposition again:

\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=m\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[\kappa_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})],\qquad\mathbf{H}=m\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{w}}]

Analogously to the development in Section
D.2.1
, we have a unique
analytical neural tangent reproducing kernel Hilbert space
(analytical NTRKHS)
\mathscr{H}
with
\kappa
as its reproducing kernel and its inner product and norm denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{H}}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}
. We also have the inclusion and sampling operators as well as their adjoints:

\iota:\mathscr{H}\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),\quad\iota^{\star}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow\mathscr{H},\quad\bm{\iota}:\mathscr{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\bm{\iota}^{\star}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}

and denoting
H\vcentcolon=\iota\circ\iota^{\star}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
, we have

Hf(\cdot)=\iota^{\star}f(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{x})\kappa(\mathbf{x},\cdot)],\qquad\bm{\iota}\circ\bm{\iota}^{\star}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbf{H}.

D.2.3
Spectral Theory for Neural Tangent Kernels

Consider
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Note that, since
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}=\lVert\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\rVert_{2}=1
, there is always an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that with respect to this basis,

\mathbf{x}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\text{where }\theta=\arccos(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

Then writing
\mathbf{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},...,w_{d})
with respect to this basis, we still have that
\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})

(
Vershynin 2018
, p.46, Proposition 3.3.2)
, and so
(w_{1},w_{2})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2})
. In polar coordinates, we have that
(w_{1},w_{2})
is distributed as
(r\cos\zeta,r\sin\zeta)
, where
r^{2}\sim\chi^{2}(2)
and
\zeta\sim\text{Unif}[-\pi,\pi]
. Now see that

\displaystyle\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\left[\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{w})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{r,\zeta}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{r\cos\zeta>0\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{r\cos\zeta\cos\theta+r\sin\zeta\sin\theta>0\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\zeta}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\cos\zeta>0\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{\cos(\zeta-\theta)>0\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{2\pi}\int^{\frac{\pi}{2}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}+\theta}d\zeta

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{2\pi}\right).

So
\kappa
is clearly a continuous function, which means that the associated RKHS
\mathscr{H}
is separable
(
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, p.130, Lemma 4.33)
. Hence, the self-adjoint operator
H=\iota\circ\iota^{\star}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
is compact
(
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, p.127, Theorem 4.27)
. Now we apply spectral theory for compact, self-adjoint operators. By
(
Weidmann 1980
, p.133, Theorem 6.7)
,
H
has at most countably many eigenvalues that can only cluster at 0, and each non-zero eigenvalue has finite multiplicity. Also, for any eigenvalue
\lambda
of
H
with eigenvector
\varphi
, we have

\lambda\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}_{2}=\langle\lambda\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{2}=\langle H\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{2}=\lVert\iota^{\star}\varphi\rVert^{2}_{2},

so
\lambda\geqslant 0
. We denote the eigenvalues in decreasing order with multiplicity by
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant...
with
\lambda_{l}\rightarrow 0
as
l\rightarrow\infty
from above, whose corresponding eigenfunctions
\varphi_{l},l=1,2,...
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})

(
Lang 1993
, p.443, Theorem 3.1)
. So by Parseval’s equality
(
Weidmann 1980
, p.38, Theorem 3.6)
, for any
f\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
, we have

f=\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}\langle f,\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l},\qquad\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{2}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\langle f,\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}^{2},\qquad Hf=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}\langle f,\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l},

which obviously has, as special cases,
H\varphi_{l}=\lambda_{l}\varphi_{l}
for all
l=1,2,...
.

For an arbitrary
L\in\mathbb{N}
and a function
f\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
, we denote by the superscript
L
in
f^{L}
the projection of
f
onto the subspace of
L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
spanned by the first
L
eigenfunctions
\varphi_{1},...,\varphi_{L}
, and we denote by
\tilde{f}^{L}
the projection of
f
onto the subspace of
L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
spanned by the remaining eigenfunctions
\varphi_{L+1},\varphi_{L+2},...
. Then we have

f^{L}=\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle f,\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l},\quad\tilde{f}^{L}=\sum^{\infty}_{l=L+1}\langle f,\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l},\quad f=f^{L}+\tilde{f}^{L},\quad\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert f^{L}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\tilde{f}^{L}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

We can also calculate the eigenvalues
\lambda_{l},l\in\mathbb{N}
explicitly. Denoting by

\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{r}=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }r=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+1\right)...\left(\frac{1}{2}+r-1\right)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+r)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{\Gamma(r)}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)}=\frac{(r-1)!}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)}&\text{for }r\geqslant 1\end{cases}

the rising factorial (Pochhammer symbol) of
\frac{1}{2}
, we expand out
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot)
as a Taylor series as follows:

\displaystyle\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\sum^{\infty}_{r=0}\frac{(\frac{1}{2})_{r}}{r!+2rr!}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2r+1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2r+2}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}.

Recall that
\rho_{d-1}
denotes the uniform distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Let us denote by
\sigma_{d-1}
the Lebesgue measure on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and by
\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert
the surface area of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, so that

\rho_{d-1}=\frac{\sigma_{d-1}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}.

In the following development of spherical harmonics theory, we mostly follow
(
Müller 1998
)
, though the key idea was borrowed from
(
Azevedo and Menegatto 2014
)
.

For
h=0,1,2,...
, denote by
P_{h}(d;\cdot)
the
Legendre polynomial
of order
h
in
d
dimensions
(
Müller 1998
, p.16, (
\mathsection
2.32))
,

P_{h}(d;z)=h!\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\sum^{\lfloor\frac{h}{2}\rfloor}_{r=0}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{r}\frac{(1-z^{2})^{r}z^{h-2r}}{r!(h-2r)!\Gamma(r+\frac{d-1}{2})},

and by
\mathcal{Y}_{h}(d)
the
space of spherical harmonics of order
h
in
d
dimensions

(
Müller 1998
, p.16, Definition 6)
. Then
\mathcal{Y}_{h}(d)
has the dimension
N(d,h)
given by
(
Müller 1998
, p.28, Exercise 6)

N(d,h)=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }h=0\\
d&\text{for }h=1\\
\frac{(2h+d-2)(h+d-3)!}{h!(d-2)!}&\text{for }h\geqslant 2\end{cases}.

With a slight abuse of notation, define the function
\kappa:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\kappa(z)=z\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(z)}{2\pi}\right)=\frac{z}{4}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{z^{2r+2}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)},

so that
\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\kappa(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
. This is clearly bounded, so we can apply the Funk-Hecke formula
(
Müller 1998
, p.30, Theorem 1)
to see that, for any spherical harmonic
Y_{h}\in\mathcal{Y}_{h}(d)
and any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have

\int\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})Y_{h}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})d\sigma_{d-1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\mu_{h}Y_{h}(\mathbf{x}),

where

\displaystyle\mu_{h}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int_{-1}^{1}P_{h}(d;z)\kappa(z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int_{-1}^{1}P_{h}(d;z)z\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(z)}{2\pi}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int^{1}_{-1}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}\left(\frac{z}{4}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{z^{2r+2}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}\right)dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4}\int^{1}_{-1}zP_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{1}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2r+2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

If we divide both sides of the Funk-Hecke formula by
\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert
, we obtain

H(Y_{h})(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})Y_{h}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]=\int\kappa(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})Y_{h}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})d\rho_{d-1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}Y_{h}(\mathbf{x}).

So for each
h=0,1,2,...
,
\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}
is an eigenvalue of
H
with multiplicity
N(d,h)
and eigenfunction
Y_{h}
. We now take a closer look at
\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}
for each value of
h
by applying the
Rodrigues rule

(
Müller 1998
, p.22, Lemma 4 & p.23, Exercise 1)
, which tells us that, for any
f\in C^{(h)}[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{-1}f(z)P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{h}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(h+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\int^{1}_{-1}f^{(h)}(z)(1-z^{2})^{h+\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{B(h,\frac{d-1}{2})}{2^{h}\Gamma(h)}\int^{1}_{-1}f^{(h)}(z)(1-z^{2})^{h+\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

We also use the following fact from
(
Müller 1998
, p.7, (
\mathsection
1.35) & (
\mathsection
1.36))
that

\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(\frac{d-1}{2})}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}B(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{1}{B(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}.

h=0
:

In this case,
P_{h}(d;z)=1
, so

\mu_{0}=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int^{1}_{-1}\frac{z}{2}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}-\frac{z\arccos(z)}{2\pi}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

Here, the first integrand
\frac{z}{2}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}
is an odd function, so the integral vanishes. For the second integral, we do integration by parts. Let

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\arccos(z)

\displaystyle\frac{du}{dz}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-z^{2}}}

\displaystyle\frac{dv}{dz}

\displaystyle=-z(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}\qquad

\displaystyle v=\frac{1}{d-1}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-1)}.

Then

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\left[\frac{\arccos(z)}{d-1}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-1)}\right]^{1}_{-1}+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi(d-1)}\int^{1}_{-1}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}d-1}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi(d-1)}B\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d}{2}\right).

Hence,

\frac{\mu_{0}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{B(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2})}{2\pi(d-1)B(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}}{2\pi(d-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}.

Here, if
d
is even, then

\frac{\mu_{0}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{\left(\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)!\right)^{2}2^{\frac{d}{2}}2^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{2\pi(d-1)\sqrt{\pi}(d-1)!!(d-3)!!\sqrt{\pi}}=\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{\pi(d-1)!!}\right)^{2},

and if
d
is odd, then

\frac{\mu_{0}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{((d-2)!!\sqrt{\pi})^{2}}{2\pi(d-1)2^{d-1}(\frac{d-1}{2})!(\frac{d-3}{2})!}=\frac{(d-2)!!}{4(d-1)(d-1)!!(d-3)!!}=\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{2(d-1)!!}\right)^{2}.

h=1
:

By applying the Rodrigues rule, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int^{1}_{-1}z\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(z)}{2\pi}\right)P_{1}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)\int^{1}_{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(z)}{2\pi}+\frac{z}{2\pi\sqrt{1-z^{2}}}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-1)}dz.

Here, in the last term, the integrand
\frac{z(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}{2\pi\sqrt{1-z^{2}}}
is an odd function, so the integral vanishes. The first term is

\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)\int^{1}_{-1}\frac{1}{2}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d-1}{2}}dz=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)B\left(\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).

The second term can be calculated by using integration by parts again:

\displaystyle-\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4\pi}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)\int^{1}_{-1}\arccos(z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d-1}{2}}dz

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4\pi}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)\frac{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)}{2\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{8}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)B\left(\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).

Hence,

\mu_{1}=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{8}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)B\left(\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right),

and so

\frac{\mu_{1}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)B\left(\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}{8B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}=\frac{1}{4d}.

h=2
:

By applying the Rodrigues rule, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{2}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert\int^{1}_{-1}P_{2}(d;z)z\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\arccos(z)}{2\pi}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert B\left(2,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{4}\int^{1}_{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{1-z^{2}}}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\pi(1-z^{2})^{3/2}}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d+1)}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert B\left(2,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{4}\int^{1}_{-1}\frac{2-z^{2}}{2\pi}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert B\left(2,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{8\pi}\int^{1}_{-1}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}+(1-z^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert B\left(2,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{8\pi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)}+\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+3}{2}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert B\left(2,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{8\pi}\left(B\left(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)+B\left(\frac{d}{2}+1,\frac{1}{2}\right)\right).

So

\frac{\mu_{2}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\frac{B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},2\right)}{8\pi B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(B\left(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)+B\left(\frac{d}{2}+1,\frac{1}{2}\right)\right).

Odd
h\geqslant 3
:

Recall that we have

\displaystyle\mu_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4}\int^{1}_{-1}zP_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{1}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2r+2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

By applying the Rodrigues rule to the first two terms, the
h^{\text{th}}
derivative vanishes, so the terms themselves vanish. By applying the Rodrigues rule to the summation term, for
r<\frac{h}{2}-1
, the derivative vanishes, and for
r\geqslant\frac{h}{2}-1
, the integrand becomes
z^{2r+2-h}(1-z^{2})^{h+\frac{d-3}{2}}
, which is an odd function since
h
is odd, so the integral vanishes. So
\mu_{h}=0
.

Even
h\geqslant 4
:

Again, recall that we have

\displaystyle\mu_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{4}\int^{1}_{-1}zP_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{r=1}\frac{1}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2r+2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

By applying the Rodrigues rule to the first two terms, the
h^{\text{th}}
derivative vanishes, so the terms themselves vanish. By applying the Rodrigues rule to the summation term, for
r<\frac{h}{2}-1
, the derivative vanishes. By applying the Rodrigues rule to
r\geqslant\frac{h}{2}-1
, we have

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2r+2}P_{h}(d;z)(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\binom{2r+2}{h}\frac{h!B(h,\frac{d-1}{2})}{2^{h}\Gamma(h)}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2r+2-h}(1-z^{2})^{h+\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\binom{2r+2}{h}\frac{hB(h,\frac{d-1}{2})}{2^{h}}\int^{1}_{0}u^{r+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{h}{2}}(1-u)^{h+\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}du

\displaystyle=\binom{2r+2}{h}\frac{hB(h,\frac{d-1}{2})}{2^{h}}B\left(r+\frac{3}{2}-\frac{h}{2},h+\frac{d-1}{2}\right).

So

\mu_{h}=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert h}{2^{h+1}\pi}B\left(h,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\sum^{\infty}_{r=\frac{h}{2}-1}\frac{\binom{2r+2}{h}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}B\left(r+\frac{3}{2}-\frac{h}{2},h+\frac{d-1}{2}\right).

To sum up, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant...
of
H
are

\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{\pi(d-1)!!}\right)^{2}\text{ for even }d\text{ and }\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{2(d-1)!!}\right)^{2}\text{ for odd }d&\text{for }h=0,\\
\frac{1}{4d}&\text{for }h=1,\\
\frac{B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},2\right)}{8\pi B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(B\left(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)+B\left(\frac{d}{2}+1,\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)&\text{for }h=2,\\
0&\text{for odd }h\geqslant 3,\\
\frac{hB\left(h,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{2^{h+1}\pi^{2}B(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}\sum^{\infty}_{r=\frac{h}{2}-1}\frac{\binom{2r+2}{h}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}B\left(r+\frac{3}{2}-\frac{h}{2},h+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)&\text{for even }h\geqslant 4,\end{cases}

with multiplicities
1
for
h=0
,
d
for
h=1
and
\frac{(2h+d-2)(h+d-3)!}{h!(d-2)!}
for
h\geqslant 2
.

D.2.4
Full-Batch Gradient Flow

Our goal is to optimize for the weight matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
using full-batch gradient flow. We perform gradient flow with respect to both the empirical risk
\mathbf{R}
and the population risk
R
, the latter obviously not possible in practice.

Note that

\nabla_{f_{W}}R(f_{W})=2(f_{W}-f^{\star})=-2\zeta_{W}\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),\quad\nabla_{\mathbf{f}_{W}}\mathbf{R}(f_{W})=\frac{2}{n}(\mathbf{f}_{W}-\mathbf{y})=-\frac{2}{n}\bm{\xi}_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Using the chain rule and results from previous sections, we calculate the gradient of the risks as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}R(f_{W})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a_{j}}{\sqrt{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\zeta_{W}(\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}R(f_{W})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{f_{W}}R,\nabla_{W}f_{W}\rangle_{2}=-2\langle G_{w},\zeta_{W}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{m}}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{W}(\mathbf{x})(\mathbf{a}\odot\phi^{\prime}(W\mathbf{x}))\mathbf{x}^{\top}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\mathbf{R}(f_{W})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a_{j}}{n\sqrt{m}}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\bm{\xi}_{W}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}\mathbf{R}(f_{W})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{f}_{W}}\mathbf{R},\nabla_{W}\mathbf{f}_{W}\rangle_{2}=-\frac{2}{n}\mathbf{G}_{W}\bm{\xi}_{W}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n\sqrt{m}}(\text{diag}[\mathbf{a}]\phi^{\prime}(WX^{\top}))*X^{\top})\bm{\xi}_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}.

For
t\geqslant 0
, denote by
W(t)
and
\hat{W}(t)
the weight matrix at time
t
obtained by gradient flow with respect to
R
and
\mathbf{R}
respectively. They both start at random initialization
W(0)
as in Section
D.2.2
, and are updated as follows:

\frac{dW}{dt}=-\nabla_{W}R(f_{W(t)})=2\langle G_{W(t)},\zeta_{W(t)}\rangle_{2},\qquad\frac{d\hat{W}}{dt}=-\nabla_{W}\mathbf{R}(f_{\hat{W}(t)})=\frac{2}{n}\mathbf{G}_{\hat{W}(t)}\bm{\xi}_{\hat{W}(t)}.

For conciseness of notation, we denote the dependence on
W(t)
and
\hat{W}(t)
simply by the subscript
t
and the hat
\hat{}
. So we write
f_{t}
and
\hat{f}_{t}
for
f_{W(t)}
and
f_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\mathbf{f}_{t}
and
\hat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}
for
\mathbf{f}_{W(t)}
and
\mathbf{f}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
J_{t}
and
\hat{J}_{t}
for
J_{W(t)}
and
J_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\mathbf{J}_{t}
and
\hat{\mathbf{J}}_{t}
for
\mathbf{J}_{W(t)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
G_{t}
and
\hat{G}_{t}
for
G_{W(t)}
and
G_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\mathbf{G}_{t}
and
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{t}
for
\mathbf{G}_{W(t)}
and
\mathbf{G}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\kappa_{t}
and
\hat{\kappa}_{t}
for
\kappa_{W(t)}
and
\kappa_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\iota_{t}
and
\hat{\iota}_{t}
for
\iota_{W(t)}
and
\iota_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\bm{\iota}_{t}
and
\hat{\bm{\iota}}_{t}
for
\bm{\iota}_{W(t)}
and
\bm{\iota}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
H_{t}
and
\hat{H}_{t}
for
H_{W(t)}
and
H_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\mathbf{H}_{t}
and
\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{t}
for
\mathbf{H}_{W(t)}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{t,1}\geqslant...\geqslant\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{t,n}=\hat{\bm{\lambda}}_{t,\text{min}}
for
\bm{\lambda}_{\hat{W}(t),1}\geqslant...\geqslant\bm{\lambda}_{\hat{W}(t),n}=\bm{\lambda}_{\hat{W}(t),\text{min}}
,
\xi_{t}
and
\hat{\xi}_{t}
for
\xi_{W(t)}
and
\xi_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\bm{\xi}_{t}
and
\hat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}
for
\bm{\xi}_{W(t)}
and
\bm{\xi}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\zeta_{t}
and
\hat{\zeta}_{t}
for
\zeta_{W(t)}
and
\zeta_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
\bm{\zeta}_{t}
and
\hat{\bm{\zeta}}_{t}
for
\bm{\zeta}_{W(t)}
and
\bm{\zeta}_{\hat{W}(t)}
,
R_{t}
and
\hat{R}_{t}
for
R(f_{t})
and
R(\hat{f}_{t})
, and
\mathbf{R}_{t}
and
\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{t}
for
\mathbf{R}(f_{t})
and
\mathbf{R}(\hat{f}_{t})
(see Table
2
).

Using the chain rule, we can also calculate the time derivative of the networks
f_{t}
and
\hat{f}_{t}
, as well as the empirical evaluation
\hat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}
of
\mathbf{f}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\frac{df_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)=-\frac{d\xi_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)=-\frac{d\zeta_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla_{W}f_{t}(\cdot),\frac{dW}{dt}\right\rangle_{\text{F}}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle G_{t}(\cdot),\langle G_{t},\zeta_{t}\rangle_{2}\right\rangle_{\text{F}}

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\langle G_{t}(\cdot),G_{t}(\mathbf{x})\rangle_{\text{F}}\zeta_{t}(\mathbf{x})]

\displaystyle=2H_{t}\zeta_{t}(\cdot)\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{f}_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)=-\frac{d\hat{\xi}_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)=-\frac{d\hat{\zeta}_{t}}{dt}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla_{W}\hat{f}_{t}(\cdot),\frac{d\hat{W}}{dt}\right\rangle_{\text{F}}=\frac{2}{n}\left\langle\hat{G}_{t}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{t}\hat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}\right\rangle_{\text{F}}\in L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{f}_{t}}{dt}=-\frac{d\bm{\xi}_{t}}{dt}=-\frac{d\bm{\zeta}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{W}\mathbf{f}_{t}\right)^{\top}\text{vec}\left(\frac{dW}{dt}\right)=2\mathbf{G}_{t}^{\top}\text{vec}\left(\langle G_{t},\zeta_{t}\rangle_{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}}{dt}=-\frac{d\hat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}}{dt}=-\frac{d\hat{\bm{\zeta}}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{W}\hat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}\right)^{\top}\text{vec}\left(\frac{d\hat{W}}{dt}\right)=\frac{2}{n}\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{t}\hat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}=\frac{2}{n}\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{t}\hat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Define
W^{L}(0)=W(0)
and
\tilde{W}^{L}(0)=0
, so that
W^{L}(0)+\tilde{W}^{L}(0)=W(0)
. See that

R_{t}=\lVert\zeta_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+R(f^{\star})=\lVert\zeta^{L}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\tilde{\zeta}^{L}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+R(f^{\star})

where we used the
\zeta^{L}_{t}=\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle\zeta_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}
and
\tilde{\zeta}^{L}_{t}=\sum^{\infty}_{l=L+1}\langle\zeta_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}
notation from Section
D.2.3
. We denote the gradients of
f^{L}_{t}
and
\tilde{f}^{L}_{t}
with respect to the weights as

G^{L}_{t}=\nabla_{W}f^{L}_{t},\qquad\tilde{G}^{L}_{t}=\nabla_{W}\tilde{f}^{L}_{t}.

Then we can see that

G^{L}_{t}=\nabla_{W}\left(\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle f_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}\right)=\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle\nabla_{W}f_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}=\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle G_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}

so that

\displaystyle\kappa^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\langle G^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x}),G^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle G_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}(\mathbf{x}),\sum^{L}_{l^{\prime}=1}\langle G_{t},\varphi_{l^{\prime}}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\text{F}}

\displaystyle=\sum^{L}_{l,l^{\prime}=1}\varphi_{l}(\mathbf{x})\varphi_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\left\langle\langle G_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2},\langle G_{t},\varphi_{l^{\prime}}\rangle_{2}\right\rangle_{\text{F}}

We also denote the projected risks as

R^{L}_{t}=\lVert\zeta^{L}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{2}+R(f^{\star})\qquad\tilde{R}^{L}_{t}=\lVert\tilde{\zeta}^{L}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+R(f^{\star}),

so that their gradients with respect to the weights are

\nabla_{W}R^{L}_{t}=-2\langle G^{L}_{t},\zeta^{L}_{t}\rangle_{2},\qquad\nabla_{W}\tilde{R}^{L}_{t}=-2\langle\tilde{G}^{L}_{t},\tilde{\zeta}^{L}_{t}\rangle_{2}

and we have

\nabla_{W}R_{t}=\nabla_{W}R^{L}_{t}+\nabla_{W}\tilde{R}^{L}_{t}.

Then we perform gradient flow on each of the projections as follows:

\frac{dW^{L}}{dt}=-\nabla_{W}R^{L}_{t}=2\langle G^{L}_{t},\zeta^{L}_{t}\rangle_{2},\qquad\frac{d\tilde{W}^{L}}{dt}=-\nabla_{W}\tilde{R}^{L}_{t}=2\langle\tilde{G}^{L}_{t},\tilde{\zeta}^{L}_{t}\rangle_{2},

Then by using the decomposition of
\nabla_{W}R_{t}=\nabla_{W}R^{L}_{t}+\nabla_{W}\tilde{R}^{L}_{t}
from above, we can see that, for
t\geqslant 0
,

W(t)=\int^{t}_{0}\frac{dW}{dt}dt=\int^{t}_{0}\frac{dW^{L}}{dt}+\frac{d\tilde{W}^{L}}{dt}dt=W^{L}(t)+\tilde{W}^{L}(t).

For individual neurons in
W^{L}(t)
, write
\mathbf{w}^{L}_{j}(t)
, and likewise
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{L}_{j}(t)
for individual neurons in
\tilde{W}^{L}(t)
.

We define
\kappa^{L}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\kappa^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\langle G^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x}),G^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}.

Moreover, we denote the RKHS associated with
\kappa^{L}_{t}
as
\mathscr{H}^{L}_{t}
, the associated inclusion operator as
\iota^{L}_{t}:\mathscr{H}^{L}_{t}\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
and the associated operator as

H^{L}_{t}=\iota^{L}_{t}\circ(\iota^{L}_{t})^{\star}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1}),\qquad H^{L}_{t}f(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[\kappa^{L}_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})f(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})].

It must be stressed that
f_{t}^{L}=\sum^{L}_{l=1}\langle f_{t},\varphi_{l}\rangle_{2}\varphi_{l}
is not necessarily the same as
f_{W^{L}(t)}
. Similarly,
G^{L}_{t}
,
\kappa^{L}_{t}
and
H^{L}_{t}
are not necessarily the same as
\nabla_{W}f_{W^{L}(t)}
,
\kappa_{W^{L}(t)}
and
H_{W^{L}(t)}
.

D.3
High Probability Results

Before we dive into our proofs, we first remark that our results are high-probability results, and the randomness comes from the sampling randomness of the data
\{\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
(or
X
and
\mathbf{y}
) and the random initialization of the neurons
\{\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\}_{j=1}^{m}
(or the weight matrix
W(0)
). Since we are performing full-batch, deterministic gradient flow, once those are fixed, the trajectory of gradient flow is completely deterministic. Hence, it is often done in the literature that first all the results that hold on a single high-probability event are proved, and then those that follow in a deterministic way on this high-probability event are proved. In the literature, this is variously called “quasi-randomness”
(
Razborov 2022
, Section 3.1)
, a “good run”
(
Frei et al. 2022
, Definition 4.4)
or a “good event”
(
Xu and Gu 2023
, Section 4.1)
.

We also collect some high-probability results in this section. Then, overfitting, approximation and estimation results in Appendix
D.4
, Appendix
D.5
and Appendix
D.6
are proved in a deterministic fashion conditioned on the high-probability event of this section. Each of the high-probability results Lemmas
16
,
17
and
18
will yield a (high-probability) sub-event of the one produced by the previous result, and they will be denoted as
E_{1}\supseteq E_{2}\supseteq E_{3}
. Our final event on which all of our result hold will have probability
1-\delta
, where
\delta
is the failure probability.

We start by collecting some preliminary non-random results that will be used throughout.

Lemma 15
.

We have the following results.

1.

The operator norm of
H:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
is given by

\lVert H\rVert_{2}=\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{4d}.

2.

For any weights
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
, we have

\lVert H_{W}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2d},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lVert H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2md}.

As a result, we also have, for all
t\geqslant 0
,

\lVert\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}R_{t}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2}{md}}\lVert\zeta_{t}\rVert_{2}.

3.

We have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\left[(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{d}.

Proof.

1.

Recall from Section
D.2.3
that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant...
of
H
are

\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{\pi(d-1)!!}\right)^{2}\text{ for even }d\text{ and }\left(\frac{(d-2)!!}{2(d-1)!!}\right)^{2}\text{ for odd }d&h=0,\\
\frac{1}{4d}&h=1,\\
\frac{B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},2\right)}{8\pi B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(B\left(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)+B\left(\frac{d}{2}+1,\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)&h=2,\\
0&\text{odd }h\geqslant 3,\\
\frac{hB\left(h,\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}{2^{h+1}\pi^{2}B(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}\sum^{\infty}_{r=\frac{h}{2}-1}\frac{\binom{2r+2}{h}}{B(\frac{1}{2},r)r(1+2r)}B\left(r+\frac{3}{2}-\frac{h}{2},h+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)&\text{even }h\geqslant 4,\end{cases}

with multiplicities
1
for
h=0
,
d
for
h=1
and
\frac{(2h+d-2)(h+d-3)!}{h!(d-2)!}
for
h\geqslant 2
.

Clearly, the values of
\frac{\mu_{h}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}
for even
h\geqslant 2
are smaller than those for
h=0
and
h=1
. Moreover, see that, when
d
is odd, using the elementary inequality
\frac{a}{a+1}<\sqrt{\frac{a}{a+2}}
,

\frac{(d-2)!!}{(d-1)!!}=\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{d-4}{d-3}...\frac{3}{4}\frac{1}{2}<\sqrt{\frac{d-2}{d}}\sqrt{\frac{d-4}{d-2}}...\sqrt{\frac{3}{5}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}},

and when
d
is even, using the same elementary inequality,

\frac{(d-2)!!}{\pi(d-1)!!}=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{d-4}{d-3}...\frac{4}{5}\frac{2}{3}<\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{d-2}{d}}...\sqrt{\frac{4}{6}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{4}}<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}.

Hence, we always have that
\frac{\mu_{0}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}<\frac{\mu_{1}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}
, and so
\lambda_{1}=...=\lambda_{d}=\frac{1}{4d}
, and
\lambda_{d+1}=\frac{\mu_{0}}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}
.

Finally, since
H
is a self-adjoint (and therefore a normal) operator on
L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
, the operator norm of
H
coincides with the spectral radius
(
Weidmann 1980
, p.127, Theorem 5.44)
, meaning that

\lVert H\rVert_{2}=\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{4d}.

2.

We define linear operators
\Xi,\tilde{\Xi}:L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\rho_{d-1})
by

\Xi(f)(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}f(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})],\qquad\tilde{\Xi}(f)(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}f(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})].

Notice that
H_{W}
is given as the integral operator of the NTK
\kappa_{W}
, which in turn is a tensor product of the dot product kernel, which is the associated kernel of
\Xi
, and the kernel
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
. Since the second kernel is bounded above by 1, Lemma
12
tells us that

\lVert H_{W}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\lVert\Xi\rVert_{2},\qquad\lVert H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\lVert\tilde{\Xi}\rVert_{2}.

Now, since
\Xi
and
\tilde{\Xi}
are self-adjoint (and therefore normal) operators, their operator norms are equal to their largest eigenvalues. We now use the Funk-Hecke formula
(
Müller 1998
, p.30, Theorem 1)
again to see that the eigenvalues
\tau_{h}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{h}
of
\Xi
and
\tilde{\Xi}
are given by

\tau_{h}=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}\int^{1}_{-1}P_{h}(d;z)z(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz.

Here, note that
P_{0}(d;z)=1
, so for
h=0
, the integrand is an odd function, which gives
\tau_{0}=0
. Moreover, using the Rodrigues rule, we can see that
\tau_{h}=0
for
h\geqslant 2
, because the
h^{\text{th}}
derivative of
z
is zero. Hence, using the Rodrigues rule, we can see that

\displaystyle\lVert H_{W}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert\Xi\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}\int^{1}_{-1}z^{2}(1-z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(d-3)}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\rvert}{2\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\rvert}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},1\right)B\left(\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2d}.

Similarly, we have

\lVert H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\lVert\tilde{\Xi}\rVert_{2}=\tilde{\tau}_{1}=\frac{1}{2md}.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}R_{t}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=2\lVert\langle G_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)},\zeta_{t}\rangle_{2}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=2\lVert\mathbb{E}[G_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)}(\mathbf{x})\zeta_{t}(\mathbf{x})]\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\left[\left(G_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)}(\mathbf{x})\cdot G_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\zeta_{t}(\mathbf{x})\zeta_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right]}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\left\langle\zeta_{t},H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)}\zeta_{t}\right\rangle_{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\lVert\zeta_{t}\rVert_{2}\sqrt{\lVert H_{\mathbf{w}_{j}(t)}\rVert_{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2}{md}}\lVert\zeta_{t}\rVert_{2}

as required.

3.

See that
\sqrt{d}\mathbf{x}
and
\sqrt{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
are independent isotropic random vectors
(
Vershynin 2018
, p.45, Exercise 3.3.1)
, so by
(
Vershynin 2018
, p.44, Lemma 3.2.4)
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2}]=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[((\sqrt{d}\mathbf{x})\cdot(\sqrt{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))^{2}]=\frac{1}{d^{2}}d=\frac{1}{d},

as required.

∎

D.3.1
Randomness due to Weight Initialization

We first collect a few results that weights at initialization satisfy with high probability. In these results, the only randomness comes from the weight initialization.

Lemma 16
.

If Assumption
2
1
is satisfied, there is an event
E_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\geqslant 1-\frac{\delta}{3}
on which the following happen simultaneously.

1.

The initial weights are upper bounded in norm: for all
j=1,...,m
,

\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{5d+4\log m}.

2.

The initial NTK operator concentrates to the analytical NTK operator:

\lVert H_{0}-H\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{5}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2m)}{dm}}.

3.

We have:

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{j\in\{1,...,m\}:\exists\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\textnormal{ with }\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0\textnormal{ and }\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\sqrt{dm}(34+\sqrt{\log m}).

4.

We have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{j\in\{1,...,m\}:\exists\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ with }\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0\text{ and }\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m}).

Proof.

1.

Note that, for each
j=1,...,m
,
\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}(d)
, so by (

χ
2

-1
), for any
c>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}^{2}\geqslant d+2\sqrt{dc}+2c\right)\leqslant e^{-c}.

Letting
c=d+\log m
and taking the square root, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{5d+4\log m}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{3d+2\log m+2\sqrt{d^{2}+d\log m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-d-\log m}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-d}}{m},

and taking the union bound over the neurons, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{5d+4\log m}\kern 5.0pt\text{for some }j\in\{1,...,m\}\right)\leqslant e^{-d}.

We note that
e^{-d}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{12}
by Assumption
2
1
.

2.

We start by defining, for each pair
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, a function
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})=\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}>0\}\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}>0\}.

The intuition behind the functions
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
is the following (see Figure
3
). For each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is cut into two disjoint halves by the hyperplane through the origin to which
\mathbf{x}
is a normal, which we denote by
\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
, and with
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
containing the hyperplane. If
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
, then
\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w})=1
, and if
\mathbf{w}\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
, then
\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w})=0
. For each pair
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the function
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
makes two such cuts, and thus is given by

•
0
\mathbf{x}
\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
•
0
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
•
0
\mathbb{G}^{d}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}

Figure 3
:
In the third picture, the shaded region represents
\mathbb{G}^{d}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}\cap\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
, and thus contain those
\mathbf{w}
such that
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})=\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w})\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=1
.

g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}\cap\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=\vcentcolon\mathbb{G}^{d}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\\
0&\text{if }\mathbf{w}\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}\cup\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\end{cases}.

So
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
takes value 1 for at most half of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(if
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
) and takes value 0 for the rest of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For example, if
\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=-1
, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
are diametrically opposite on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then
\mathbb{G}^{d}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
and
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
is the zero function. We also define the following collections of sets:

\mathcal{H}\vcentcolon=\left\{\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right\}\qquad\mathcal{G}\vcentcolon=\left\{\mathbb{G}^{d}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}:\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right\}.

So
\mathcal{H}
is the collection of half-spaces in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\mathcal{G}
is the collection of intersections of two half-spaces in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The
growth function

\Pi_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
of
\mathcal{G}
is defined as
(
Mohri et al. 2012
, p.38, Definition 3.3)
,
(
van de Geer 2000
, p.39, Definition 3.2)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{G}}(m)

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\left\{(g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{1}),...,g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{m})):\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\left\{\mathbb{G}\cap\{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m}\}:\mathbb{G}\in\mathcal{G}\right\}\right\rvert.

The growth function
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
of
\mathcal{H}
is similarly defined. Then by
(
van de Geer 2000
, p.40, Example 3.7.4c)
, we have

\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(m)\leqslant 2^{d}\binom{m}{d}\leqslant(2m)^{d},

and noting that
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathbb{H}_{1}\cap\mathbb{H}_{2}:\mathbb{H}_{1},\mathbb{H}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}\}
,
(
Mohri et al. 2012
, p.57, Exercise 3.15(a))
tells us that

\Pi_{\mathcal{G}}(m)\leqslant(\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(m))^{2}\leqslant(2m)^{2d}.

Now, we let
\{\varsigma_{j}\}^{m}_{j=1}
be a
Rademacher sequence
, i.e.,a sequence of independent random variables
\varsigma_{j}
with
\mathbb{P}(\varsigma_{j}=1)=\mathbb{P}(\varsigma_{k}=-1)=\frac{1}{2}
. Then using an argument based on Massart’s Lemma
(
Mohri et al. 2012
, p.40, Corollary 3.1)
, we can bound the Rademacher complexity by

\mathbb{E}_{\varsigma_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j}(0),j=1...,m}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))\right]\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2\log\Pi_{\mathcal{G}}(m)}{m}}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}}.

We also define a function
F:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})=\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left\{\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})]\right\}.

Then for any
j^{\prime}\in\{1,...,m\}
and any
\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left\{\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-\frac{1}{m}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j})+\frac{1}{m}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})]\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}-1},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}+1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{m}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}})-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}-1},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}+1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})+\frac{1}{m},

since
g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})\in\{0,1\}
. So

\lvert F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})-F(\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}-1},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},\mathbf{w}_{j^{\prime}+1},...,\mathbf{w}_{m})\rvert\leqslant\frac{1}{m}.

Hence, we can apply McDiarmid’s inequality (
B.2
) to see that, for any
c>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\geqslant\mathbb{E}[F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))]+c\right)\leqslant e^{-2c^{2}m}.

Now, to bound
\mathbb{E}[F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))]
, we use symmetrization. Denote by
\mathcal{F}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0)
. Suppose we had another set
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{m}
of independent copies from the distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Then for each pair
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))\mid\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\mid\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})],

so

\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}\right].

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\left\{\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\right],

where the last line follows from the law of iterated expectations. Then noting that

\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\text{ and }\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}

have the same distribution, continuing our argument from above,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}\left\{g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))+\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\varsigma_{j}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}},

by the bound in (*). Hence, continuing from (**), for any
c>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\geqslant 4\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}}+c\right)\leqslant e^{-2c^{2}m}.

Letting
c=\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\geqslant 5\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}}\right)\leqslant e^{-2d\log(2m)}=\frac{1}{(2m)^{2d}}.

We note that
\frac{1}{(2m)^{2d}}\leqslant e^{-d}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{12}
by Assumption
2
1
.

Now we assume we are on the above high probability event on which
F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))\leqslant 5\sqrt{\frac{d\log(2m)}{m}}
. We use the same linear operator
\Xi
as in the proof of Lemma
15
2
, which we recall to be

\Xi(f)(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}[\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}f(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

and we also recall that
\lVert\Xi\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2d}
. Applying Lemma
12
, we see that

\displaystyle\lVert H_{0}-H\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2d}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0))-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}[g_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{w})]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2d}F(\mathbf{w}_{1}(0),...,\mathbf{w}_{m}(0))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{5}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2m)}{dm}},

as required.

3.

We use the net argument. We know that, by
(
Vershynin 2018
, p.78, Corollary 4.2.13)
, the
\frac{2}{\sqrt{5d+4\log m}}\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}
-covering number of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is upper bounded by
\left(\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}}\sqrt{5d+4\log m}+1\right)^{d}
. Let
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
be such a cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Also, for each
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, define
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{z}\rvert\leqslant 34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}\right\}.

Note that, for each
j=1,...,m
and each
\mathbf{z}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, the real-valued random variable
\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)
has distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, since
\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{2}=1
and
\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. So

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant 34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int^{34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}}_{34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}dz\leqslant 34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}.

Denote by
\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}
the set of neurons that are in
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}
. This is a random set, and we clearly have

\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}\mathbf{1}_{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)).

By Hoeffding’s inequality (
B.2
), for any
c>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant 34\sqrt{dm}+c\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}-\sum^{m}_{j=1}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}\right)\geqslant c\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{2c^{2}}{m}\right).

Letting
c=\sqrt{md\log m}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\sqrt{dm}\left(34+\sqrt{\log m}\right)\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{m^{2d}}.

We take the union bound over all
\mathbf{z}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\text{there exists }\mathbf{z}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}\text{ such that }\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\sqrt{dm}\left(34+\sqrt{\log m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}}\sqrt{5d+4\log m}+1\right)^{d}\frac{1}{m^{2d}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-d}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\delta}{12},

where the last line follows by Assumption
2
1
.

Now suppose that we are on this high-probability event on which there does not exist
\mathbf{z}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\sqrt{dm}(34+\sqrt{\log m})
. Then for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, denote by
\mathbf{x}_{0}
the element in the net
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{5d+4\log m}}\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}
. Then for any
\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\notin\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathbf{z}}
, noting that part
1
tells us that
\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{5d+4\log m}
, we have

\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{x}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert-\lvert(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert>34\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}-2\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}=32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}.

Hence, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have at most
\sqrt{dm}(34+\sqrt{\log m})
neurons that satisfy
\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}
. See that, for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and each
j=1,...,m
, for there to exist a
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0
and
\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}
, a necessary condition is that
\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}
, since

\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant\lvert(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)-\mathbf{v})\cdot\mathbf{x}\rvert+\lvert\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}\rvert\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)-\mathbf{v}\rVert_{2}\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}.

Thus

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{j\in\{1,...,m\}:\exists\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ with }\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0\text{ and }\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant 32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}}\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\sqrt{dm}(34+\sqrt{\log m}).

4.

We follow a similar argument as in part
3
. We know that the
\frac{2}{\sqrt{5d+4\log m}}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
-covering number of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is upper bounded by
\left(\frac{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{5d+4\log m}+1\right)^{d}
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be such a cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Also, for each
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, define
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{z}\rvert\leqslant\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\right\}.

Note that, for each
j=1,...,m
and each
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{C}
, the real-valued random variable
\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)
has distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, since
\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{2}=1
and
\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. So

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\lvert\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int^{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}}_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}dz\leqslant\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}.

Denote by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}
the set of neurons that are in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}
. This is a random set, and we clearly have

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}}(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)).

By Hoeffding’s inequality (
B.2
), for any
c>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\frac{3\sqrt{2}\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}+c\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}-\sum^{m}_{j=1}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}\right)\geqslant c\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{2c^{2}}{m}\right).

Letting
c=\frac{\sqrt{m\log m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\left(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m}\right)\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{m^{\frac{2}{d\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}}}}.

We take the union bound over all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\text{there exists }\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{C}\text{ such that }\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\left(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{5d+4\log m}+1\right)^{d}\frac{1}{m^{\frac{2}{d\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-d}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\delta}{12},

where the last line follows by Assumption
2
1
.

Now suppose that we are on this high-probability event on which there does not exist
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{z}}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m})
. Then for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, denote by
\mathbf{x}_{0}
the element in the net
\mathcal{S}
such that
\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{5d+4\log m}}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
. Then for any
\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\notin\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{z}}
, noting that part
1
tells us that
\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{5d+4\log m}
, we have

\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{x}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert-\lvert(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert>\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}.

Hence, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we have at most
\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m})
neurons that satisfy
\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
. See that, for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and each
j=1,...,m
, for there to exist a
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0
and
\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
, a necessary condition is that
\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}
, since

\lvert\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rvert\leqslant\lvert(\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)-\mathbf{v})\cdot\mathbf{x}\rvert+\lvert\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}\rvert\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)-\mathbf{v}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}.

Thus

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{j\in\{1,...,m\}:\exists\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ with }\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0\text{ and }\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}_{j}(0)\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{md}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{d}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}(3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{\log m}).

Now, the events of parts
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
each have probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{12}
, so by union bound, the event
E_{1}
on which all of them happen simultaneously satisfies
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\geqslant 1-\frac{\delta}{3}
, as required.
∎

D.3.2
Randomness due to Sampling of Data

We now state and prove a few results that the samples satisfy with high probability. In these results, the only randomness comes from the random sampling of the training data.

Lemma 17
.

If Assumptions
2
1
&
2
are satisfied, there is an event
E_{2}\subseteq E_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\geqslant 1-\frac{2\delta}{3}
on which the following happen simultaneously.

1.

The spectral norm of the data matrix is bounded above as follows:

\lVert X\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}.

This implies that, for any weights
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
with rows
\mathbf{w}_{j},j=1,...,m
,

\lVert\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{md}},\qquad\lVert\mathbf{G}_{W}\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lVert\mathbf{H}_{W}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{4n}{d}.

2.

The minimum eigenvalue
\bm{\lambda}_{\min}
of the analytical NTK matrix, is bounded from below:

\bm{\lambda}_{\min}\geqslant\frac{n}{5d}.

Proof.

1.

We have that the rows of
\sqrt{d}X
are independent, and by
(
Vershynin 2018
, p.45, Exercise 3.3.1)
, each row is isotropic. Moreover, each row has mean
\mathbf{0}
, and has sub-Gaussian norm bounded by an absolute constant
C_{1}>0
independent of
d

(
Vershynin 2018
, p.53, Theorem 3.4.6)
, i.e.,
\lVert\sqrt{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leqslant C_{1}
. Hence, by
(
Vershynin 2018
, p.91, Theorem 4.6.1)
, there exists an absolute constant
C_{2}>0
such that for all
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\sqrt{d}X\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{n}+C_{2}C_{1}^{2}(\sqrt{d}+t)\right)\leqslant 2e^{-t^{2}}.

Then defining an absolute constant
C\vcentcolon=2C_{2}C^{2}_{1}
, and noting that
\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\geqslant 2C
by Assumption
2
2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert X\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}+2C_{2}C^{2}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\lVert\sqrt{d}X\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{n}+2\sqrt{d}C_{2}C^{2}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=2e^{-d}

\displaystyle\text{letting }t=\sqrt{d}\text{ above}.

We note that
2e^{-d}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{6}
by Assumption
2
1
.

For the next assertions on the high-probability event that
\lVert X\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}*X^{\top})^{\top}(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}*X^{\top})\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}})\odot(XX^{\top})\rVert_{2}

by (
B.1
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert X\rVert_{2}^{2}\max_{i\in\{1,...,n\}}\lvert[\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}]_{ii}\rvert

by (
B.1
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4n}{d}\max_{i\in\{1,...,n\}}\frac{1}{m}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})^{2}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{by the above bound on }\lVert X\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4n}{dm}

\displaystyle\text{since }\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})^{2}\leqslant 1,

and by the same argument,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{G}_{W}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{J}_{W}*X^{\top})^{\top}(\mathbf{J}_{W}*X^{\top})\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{J}_{W}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{W})\odot(XX^{\top})\rVert_{2}

by (
B.1
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert X\rVert_{2}^{2}\max_{i\in\{1,...,n\}}\lvert[\mathbf{J}_{W}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{W}]_{ii}\rvert

by (
B.1
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4n}{d}\max_{i\in\{1,...,n\}}\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{j=1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})^{2}\qquad
